These layers are retained as the text’s own vocabulary. 螟蛉 refers to an adopted heir; the line does not supply a biological prediction. “Guard against the wind” preserves the flower-and-fruit image for vulnerable offspring.

[^39]: **Two damaged titles.** S’s 鄭岳侍即 is read as Vice Minister Zheng Yue, with G614’s 侍郎 supporting the repair. The Xinwei/Gengzi case has 火𠫵 in S; G identifies the office as 參政, supporting a senior provincial administrative title rather than an invented “Fire” office. Zhou Woqiao’s son Yanru is the Grand Secretary mentioned in the note; the year and month belong to the father’s chart.

[^40]: **The text excludes its own apparent examples from Rat Nobility.** The small note explicitly says that days seated upon You or Si require another configuration. Their favorable verdicts therefore should not be used to erase the Rat-Nobility exclusions. “Azure and Red” preserves 青赤, the Wood/Fire imagery. S’s poor Xinhai/Jiawu case differs from G614’s Xinhai/Jiazi; S’s month remains.

[^41]: **Later life, not necessarily Wei.** S has 未中 in the short summary, while G614 has 末中, supporting the temporal reading “middle and later life.” The final poem’s sequence 庚申巳酉丑 is read as Geng and Shen, followed by Si, You, Chou. This agrees with the corresponding prose exclusions; it is not a claim that the complete Gengshen pillar alone is prohibited.

[^42]: **A damaged connective and an institutional term.** S’s 桂 after 偏官 in the Yimao entry is read with G614’s 柱, “pillars.” 蕭注兩制 is rendered Xiao Zhu in the two drafting offices: the phrase concerns the drafting of imperial documents, not two chart restraints. S gives 李允昌, Li Yunchang, whereas G614’s rendered text has 李引昌; S’s given name is retained.

[^43]: **A traditional astral name.** S has 磨蝎; G614 reads 磨羯, supporting the intended 摩羯, Capricorn. The bracketed rendering records that repair rather than interpreting the scorpion graph as Scorpio. This inherited astrological name does not establish a modern tropical Sun sign for everyone born at Dingchou hour. The source does not explain the correspondence here.

[^44]: **The source’s own designations govern.** The Yimao verdict says 午月印生, “Wu month, Seal’s generation,” although the section’s ordinary stem relationships would not make Wu an obvious Water Seal. The text is not silently changed to a different month. The two Guimao/Yimao cases labelled great distinction and wealth are both retained. Ouyang Wenzhong is the appellation printed in S, not a newly supplied personal name.

[^45]: **You’s position, not You Earth.** S has 酉土辛重; G614 has 酉上辛重, supporting “heavy Xin at You.” The general prose calls Si–You–Chou months ordinary and then includes Chou among auspicious Earth months. Both verdicts remain, without a conjectural deletion of Chou.

[^46]: **Later catalogue annotations.** The Chongzhen entry says entry into Bing luck, not simply a Bing year. Li Chengliang’s note reads 始禍, “calamity begins”; the event and timing are not further supplied. The unnamed clerk sharing Ding Bin’s chart is retained as a separate social outcome. Such comparisons and later entries belong to the transmitted catalogue’s layers.

[^47]: **Wealth-Horse restored.** S reads 食神則馬; G614 gives 食神財馬, supporting “[Wealth-Horse].” Horse here is the Wealth metaphor and should not automatically be equated with the Traveling-Horse sha. The tiger list in the following song evokes an examination list, not an additional natal indicator.

[^48]: **An inverted phrase and a compressed line.** 水白金清 is rendered Water white, Metal clear, retaining the source’s order rather than normalizing it to the common 金白水清. 壬從辛酉通官貴 is also transmitted by G614 and remains syntactically compressed; its Ren, Xin, and You are not replaced by a newly formulated rule. The prose distinguishes actual transformation into Metal from untransformed Officer-and-Seal analysis.

[^49]: **Alternative charts remain visible.** Tang Shunzhi’s S annotation cites 星案 for a Yihai day rather than the present Yiwei day. It is retained as an alternative, not substituted into this section. Qiao Yu also appears elsewhere under a different day–hour combination; the translation does not manufacture a single reconciled natal record. The Li Chunfang annotation concerns the son of the woman whose chart is given.

[^50]: **The closing danger is not suppressed.** The song places Wu–Gui Fire and southern Li beside a Metal-transformation discussion. The final 富貴之中當險, danger amid wealth and distinction, is retained. 乾元 preserves its cosmological Qian-origin wording; the text supplies no separate palace-calculation procedure here.

[^51]: **Zhou Chong and Yi-Mao.** S’s rare name graph 𤥣 is read with G614’s 周珫 as Zhou Chong. S’s 乙𫑗 in the next year is read as Yimao, supported by G’s 乙卯. He Wanzuo’s 名宦 refers to recognition as an exemplary official, not simply personal celebrity. His subsequent Fujian offices are separate from his initial Shandong education post.

[^52]: **Shi Miyuan’s year differs.** S gives Jiayin/Bingyin; G614 says his month alone differs from Jia Yong’s Jiashen/Bingzi chart, implying Jiashen/Bingyin. S’s Jiayin is retained. The note illustrates why apparently shared historical names do not authorize silently merging the chart catalogues.

[^53]: **Tuan versus Pu.** S has 張漙, Zhang Tuan, while G614 has 張溥, Zhang Pu. The main text retains S’s graph and pronunciation rather than asserting that identification as established. The general 金木交爭, Metal and Wood contending, does not make every named person’s moral character a verified consequence of the chart.

[^54]: **Yi’s Birth at Wu.** The section uses the reversed life-cycle sequence of the yin stems. Its Yi Birth at Wu should not be corrected to the yang Wood Birth at Hai. Food Ding and Wealth Ji flourish at Wu in the same explanation. The later heading and verse continue this framework.

[^55]: **The compiler’s personal judgments remain.** S calls Qin Hui 老賊, “old villain,” and emphasizes Hai Rui’s forthrightness. These are the catalogue’s evaluations, not neutral modern biographical findings. In the Yichou list S gives the vice general Wuyin/Jiwei; G614 has Yìwèi, 乙未, for the month. S’s Jiwei, 己未, is retained.

[^56]: **An unfinished Renwu event.** The flattened Zhang Zongheng entry contains 壬午年被, “in Renwu year, was subjected to…,” without the following event. Its Renyin month is separately recoverable from the lower row. The missing verb or outcome is not fabricated, and the Renwu date is not made into a second natal month.

[^57]: **An alternate year without an alternate month.** Lin Jun’s record is followed by 一丁巳年貴同, a Dingsi-year case likewise distinguished. S supplies no separate month for it, so none has been silently inherited. The repeated Yihai/Jichou entries under Yiwei, labelled prefect and rich, are both retained.

[^58]: **Reciprocal control in the prose.** Ji Earth damaging Gui and Gui overturning Ding Food are both stated. The title 倒傷 in the opening poem is rendered “inverted injury,” not silently replaced by 倒食, although Inverted Food is the explanatory category in the prose. The monarch’s spouse in the first catalogue is the Tianqi empress, not the emperor’s own chart.

[^59]: **Inherited favor and examination rank are different paths.** The person who shared Mao Zan’s chart obtained appointment through 恩蔭, inherited favor or protection, whereas Mao is called palace-examination first. The distinction remains. The name 茅瓚 is preserved as Mao Zan; it has not been expanded into an unprinted personal biography.

[^60]: **A gloss embedded in the catalogue.** The final sentences following the Yiyou cases are technical commentary, not another person’s biography. They distinguish pure You following Killings, Si–Chou forming a Killings configuration, and Zi–Chen transforming into Seal. The translation separates this gloss visibly from the recorded cases. At Yihai, the explicit Void warning concerns the hour, not a claim that all Jiashen hours universally lack children.

[^61]: **Ghost Extinction and bodily Extinction are not silently exchanged.** The opening poem says 鬼絕壽傷; the prose says 身絕鬼旺. G614 transmits both. Their different subjects remain, rather than repairing the verse solely to match the prose. The section also alternates between transformed Metal and untransformed Wood; a favorable rule under one frame cannot automatically be transferred to the other.

[^62]: **Different outcomes under the same complete chart.** G614 makes explicit the flattened S note: Consort Zhang has four Yiyou pillars, shared by a pure and lofty person and a county magistrate. The Yuan Shizu entry instead has Yihai year and Yiyou month. All records are kept distinct. The warning against Officer luck is preserved despite the favorable transformed-Metal verdict.

[^63]: **A small supplied verb.** S leaves a space in 遷宗　祖; G614 gives 遷宗改祖, “change clan and ancestral establishment.” The bracketed English follows that supported restoration. The closing claim of no sorrow is not substituted for the earlier warnings of illness, injured relatives, and short life.

[^64]: **An unresolved future in one witness, an attained office in another.** S says Meng’s outcome is 未定, not yet settled, although it already heads his case with a chief-censor title. G614 instead gives 孟則都憲, “Meng was chief censor.” Both preserve the comparison with Liu Dashi and Qin Liang and the explanation through regional differences. S’s uncertainty is translated rather than silently updated from G. The three sexagenary examination dates are not part of the shared natal chart.

[^65]: **Jiaxu restored from G.** S has 己戌, an impossible sexagenary pairing. G614 gives 甲戌 followed by 庚午, supporting “[Jiaxu] / Gengwu.” This is a witness-supported repair, not a reconstructed historical birth date.

[^66]: **An expressly transmitted alternative hour.** Zhang Fujing’s annotation gives Bingzi as another account of the hour. It is retained here and can be compared with his Bingzi-hour entry above; neither is silently removed. 主血疾 is a general historical “blood disorder,” not an identified modern diagnosis. The source’s factual certainty is not medical evidence.

[^67]: **Earth and “no Wealth.”** The opening poem says 月氣水土無財貴, retaining Earth alongside the absence of Wealth. The prose later calls Wu Wealth and warns of Wealth damaging Seal. The translation leaves that tension visible rather than changing 土 to 木 simply to produce a smoother doctrine.

[^68]: **Hai Rui appears with two charts.** S supplies Guiyou/Xinyou under Yisi/Dinghai here, and Jiaxu/Dingchou under Yimao/Renwu earlier. Both entries name Hai Rui and emphasize his integrity. Without an independent collation sufficient to settle the record, neither chart is declared authentic at the expense of the other. The title 教論 in the preceding Yimao case is understood as a school-instruction office; the graph itself does not establish an otherwise unknown rank.

[^69]: **Names and offices follow S.** 王文焯 is rendered Wang Wenzhuo; the rare given-name graph is not normalized to an assumed historical identity. The Yichou/Dinghai prefect record and the fuller Wu Yuanyou record are both retained. The latter’s 直諒 describes forthrightness and trustworthiness; it is not a second office. “Lost five wives” renders the prognostic 克五妻 without implying demonstrated personal culpability.

[^70]: **Birth at Death, not an erased contradiction.** The general explanation says Yi dies at Hai and is revived through Ren Seal; the later poem says Food God and Yi Wood encounter Birth. Both statements are translated. The text’s distinct uses of ordinary phases, reversed yin life stages, and support from another influence should not be flattened into a single unqualified stage label.

[^71]: **Inherited and examination distinctions.** 衍聖公 is the hereditary Duke for Perpetuating the Sage, not an examination rank. 官生 indicates a student or appointee by virtue of an official father; in the Bingwu list he becomes prefect. Master Yan’s 亞聖, Second Sage, is the source’s honorific attribution. These catalogue statements do not independently establish the ancient figure’s natal chart.

[^72]: **Shen month restored.** S has 甲月, Jia month, in a sequence discussing monthly branches and Triple Combination with Killings. G614 gives 申月, supporting “[Shen].” This is not an arbitrary doctrinal change. S’s Xinyou/Dingyou prefect case under Bingwu differs from G614’s Xinyou/Guiyou, and S’s month is retained.

[^73]: **One natal pair, different examination and death years.** The Dingyou/Renzi row supplies Fang Jiugong and Zhong Huamin with one day–hour and year–month chart. Jiazi/Yichou and Jimao/Gengchen are their respective examination dates; Wuzi and Jiawu are death years. They are not extra natal pillars. This long comparison is present in S and absent from the corresponding G614 catalogue.

[^74]: **“Until Wu or Ding appears.”** The compressed 午丁未遇且沉埋 is read with 未遇 as “not yet encountered,” rather than turning 丁未 into an additional Dingwei pillar. Both S and G614 transmit the sequence. The opening Bingzi-specific wording is retained even though the song concludes a section on all six Bing days.

[^75]: **Brightness above Earth.** 明出地上 is the source’s named image; it also evokes the language of the Changes, but the short text gives no independent trigram procedure here. The first poem’s “without seeking Wealth and Lu” is left as transmitted, not converted into a prohibition on wealth itself. The prose’s welcome to Geng/Xin is conditional on Hurting Officer generating Wealth.

[^76]: **A repaired title and unchanged names.** S’s 得史 in the Jihai/Wuchen case is read “[censor]” with G614’s 御史. S’s 林欲楫 is romanized Lin Yuji, without silently changing the first given-name graph. The paired examination dates for Lin and Wang Jiayan remain distinct from their shared Bingzi/Jihai natal year and month. S specifies Chen Jin’s death in Jiazi; that date is not supplied by the shorter G record.

[^77]: **Jichou, not Yichou.** S’s individual heading reads 丙辰日乙丑時 within the Jichou section. G614 has 己丑, supporting “[Jichou].” The chapter’s hour sequence and the standard pairing also favor Ji, but the repair rests on an actual textual witness rather than calculation alone.

[^78]: **Xu rather than “become.”** In the Bingshen verdict S has 成卯貴, while G614 has 戌卯貴. “[Xu] and Mao give distinction” follows the latter. The repeated blood-disorder statement is preserved as historical prognosis, without selecting a modern disease to fill its broad category.

[^79]: **Mixed stems and branches in the conditions.** 未申癸午 is retained as Wei, Shen, Gui, Wu; 癸午 is not made into an impossible Guiwu pillar. G614 transmits the same sequence. Wu Shilai’s remonstrances, beating, exile, recall, and later office are all parts of S’s narrative and are translated without an independent claim to have verified the biography.

[^80]: **S’s appellations remain.** 曾從 is Zeng Cong, as in G614; it is not silently identified with another more familiar examination laureate. 費宷 is read Fei Cai, supported by G’s 寀 form. The bare Xinchou/Guisi office 即中 is read as bureau director with G614’s 郎中. S’s additional Wang Duo case is retained, not omitted because absent from the parallel’s shorter list.

[^81]: **A damaged office is not expanded by guesswork.** The Guichou/Dingsi annotation ends 甲部, literally Jia ministry. Neither this fragment nor the consulted G614 passage securely identifies the office, so the English marks it uncertain. A plausible 吏部 or 刑部 has not been silently supplied.

[^82]: **Joining is not necessarily stem transformation.** 丙庚相合 in the poem is translated “Bing and Geng join.” It is not one of the standard five heavenly-stem transformative pairings, and the translation does not change Geng to Xin to manufacture one. The White Tiger’s return and the pine-and-cypress forest are retained as images despite the prose’s separate phase analysis.

[^83]: **A damaged adjective and an uneasy favorable judgment.** S has 為人⿰巧虛詐, including an incomplete character-description mark. G614 gives 慣巧虛詐, supporting “[practiced in] cunning and deceit.” The preceding description of lust and discourtesy followed by 卻好, “nevertheless good,” remains: the text’s favorable prognostic verdict is not the same thing as moral praise. The opening 旺木雙妻 is retained rather than rewritten into a rule of balanced spouses.

[^84]: **Different names beside the same pair.** Dingyou/Yisi occurs once for a Zhang called palace first and again for Chen Dong called metropolitan first and palace third. Both are retained. The Bingchen/Renchen graduate’s removal in Yiwei is a career date, not another natal month. The small note on Blazing Upward after the Bingyin list belongs to all births on that day with the specified year/month combination.

[^85]: **Ye, not the Lai found elsewhere.** S uses the rare graph 𣋌 in this Yao’s name; G614 gives 姚曄, supporting “[Ye].” This case is not merged with the differently charted Yao Lai in the preceding section. S’s additional Chu Tie and Han Guang entries retain their printed name-graphs and are not identified through conjectural historical substitutions.

[^86]: **A supported variant for a difficult warning.** S has 雙寃, literally doubled grievance; G614 gives 讎冤, enmity and injustice. The bracketed phrase follows G. It is not read as Double Mandarin Ducks, which would require different characters. The surrounding favorable body-and-Killings balance remains alongside this adverse alternative.

[^87]: **A missing repeated word.** S stops at 身旺煞 before the next clause. G614 has 身旺煞旺, supplying the second 旺, “[flourishes].” The repair does not alter the subsequent distinction between restrained and unrestrained Indirect Officer.

[^88]: **Water, not Wood, in the triple configuration.** S has 申子辰木局; G614 reads 水局, supporting “[Water].” The same passage contains S’s additional Ni Yuanlu example, which is retained. Qian Zhong’s name is 錢鍾 in S but 錢錞 in G; the translation keeps S’s reading without resolving the individual’s identity by assumption.

[^89]: **Conflicting exclusions are not silently reconciled.** The opening poem excludes Ji, Yin, Ren, and Gui in the month, while the prose welcomes a Water-strengthened Officer and gives a different list of interfering positions. The translation keeps both. Where S writes 巳 for 己 beside Ren in the prose, Ji is the contextual stem reading; the separate Yin/Hai clause concerns branch punishment and clash.

[^90]: **Kuigang used beyond its narrow day list.** The Bingchen entry itself calls this 魁罡格, although Bingchen is not one of the four Kuigang days listed earlier. It is preserved as the source’s designation, not changed to a different day or discarded as impossible. S’s Zhu Ji case has Guiwei month; G614 gives Yiwei. S governs the catalogue.

[^91]: **Returning Lu may coexist with Officer here.** The short closing verse explicitly says 逢官祿亦敷, even meeting Officer, Lu spreads abundantly. This differs from the stricter “no Officer” definition elsewhere, and the distinction is important for a reader using the book as a reference. The alternative 陸沉 in the Bingshen judgment is figurative obscurity or submerged status while still alive, not literal drowning.

[^92]: **Concealed Brilliance and a contextual repair.** 伏晶 retains the image of holding or concealing brilliance. Both S and G614 print 月上伏火氣; the translation reads 上 as the graphically close 土, Earth, in the context of the four Earth months and the preceding requirement for Earth. The brackets therefore mark a contextual conjecture, not a separately attested variant. The phrase could also be construed temporally, “in the month, concealing Fire qi”; the elemental reading is not certain.

[^93]: **The Fire contradiction is retained.** The opening requires Earth to moderate excessive Fire, yet the later quatrain welcomes Fire without punishment or breaking. S and G614 preserve both, and the translation does not replace 火 with 土 merely to align the verses. The unidentified 甲部 office in the last catalogue is marked rather than expanded into a guessed ministry.

[^94]: **An imperial title and a bare punishment label.** The Renwu/Jiyou case names Ming Guangzong with the Taichang reign title; these are two designations of the single person named by S, not two chart entries. The last Dingyou/Gengxu case says only 刑人, a person subjected to punishment. It does not establish a particular crime or judicial outcome.

[^95]: **The final song welcomes what the opening warns against.** 身強財旺, a strong body and flourishing Wealth, is retained in the song although the general paragraph warns that Wealth damages Seal. A conditional explanation may be possible, but the text does not supply it at that point. No missing negative or new favorable-element doctrine has been inserted.

[^96]: **Following Wealth and arching are separate interpretations.** Pure Metal in the Bingzi entry may be judged as abandoning the Fate to follow Wealth, while the Bingxu entry can be judged as arching only when the named positions remain unfilled. The latter’s 列祖/祖 wording concerns the ancestral establishment, not a separate celestial body. Its final 甲部 office is unresolved, as in other case grids.

[^97]: **A difficult closing song.** Both S and G614 have 偏官榮旺是和非 and the final 資財虛名薄利. The English preserves the right/wrong contrast and the shift from household prosperity to empty reputation and slight gain. The song has not been rewritten as an unqualified promise of wealth merely because the preceding small verse sounds favorable.

[^98]: **Blade at the stem level.** The prose calls Ding itself Blade and says Ding is born at You while Bing dies there. This should not be corrected by substituting the ordinary branch location of Bing’s Yang Blade. The requirement that Blade receive restraint or disruptive contact is part of this account, rather than a universal judgment that all clashes are favorable.

[^99]: **The sunset image.** Bing is compared to the sun after setting at You, while the general discussion uses the technical Death stage. These poetic and technical descriptions coexist. The last 子午傍人嗔恨 retains Zi and Wu and other people’s resentment; it does not specify a new month, a geographic journey, or a remedial action.

[^100]: **A shared chart does not mean a shared career.** S compares Zhu Heng with Li Tinglong, giving Li’s lower office, Bingzi death year, and the different regions Jing and Wu. The causal explanation is the source’s. It is not an independent finding that birthplace accounts for all differences between identical charts. The two Zhang Xian ministerial cases earlier in this section likewise remain separately charted.

[^101]: **Two supported monthly repairs.** S’s 干月 in the Bingchen entry is G614’s 午月, supporting “[Wu] month.” S’s 亥未辰戊 in the Bingwu entry is G614’s 亥未辰戌, supporting Hai, Wei, Chen, “[Xu].” Neither repair changes a valid recorded natal pair. The brackets keep the interventions visible.

[^102]: **An obscure adverse line retained.** 夭孤父母早淹連 is translated with its early-death, solitude, and parental adversity language intact. G614 transmits the same wording, which does not establish whether the first terms are a separate judgment or modify the parents. The surrounding favorable lines are no reason to omit the difficult line.

[^103]: **Correct Ghost is not Direct Officer.** 正鬼 is rendered “proper ghost”; Ren is still the same-polarity controlling influence for Bing. Ji is described as 傷煞, injuring Killings, while Jia is Inverted Food. The compact 亥上有明己旺壬生甲 is punctuated to retain visible Ji, flourishing Ren, and generated Jia, without asserting Ji’s own seasonal flourishing at Hai.

[^104]: **Wu restored in a heavenly-stem clause.** S prints 天干更透丙戌字; G614 gives 丙戊, supporting Bing and “[Wu]” exposed in the heavenly stems. Xu is a branch and would be anomalous in that syntactic position. The correction is witness-supported, not a change to any catalogue’s Bingxu pair. The source’s statements about nayin and Fire/Earth transformation are retained as distinct from ordinary stem phases.

[^105]: **One chart, different lives.** The Dinghai/Guimao entry names Gao Wenjian and Tao Dalin together, records a Guiyou death, and invokes the different regions Wu and Liang. The compressed caption does not securely assign every biographical clause to one man rather than the other; the translation therefore says that the note records the death, not that both men died in that year. The regional explanation is the catalogue’s, not independently demonstrated causation.

[^106]: **The song’s stages are not silently recalculated.** S and G615 both read 火胎金絕, Fire at Embryo and Metal at Extinction. The opening prose instead calls Ding at Zi extinguished. The book alternates between stem-specific stages, phase stages, and other frameworks; the translation preserves the wording rather than making the song agree by substituting a modern table.

[^107]: **A supported heading repair, and a separate verse variant.** S’s heading ends 時忌無論, apparently saying that the hour’s prohibitions are not discussed; G615 reads 上時亦併論, judging them together with the preceding hour rule. The bracketed English follows that positive instruction, consistent with the earlier heading-level qualifications. In the opening poem, however, S’s 不為凶, “not thereby ominous,” is retained: G615 has 不為佳, “not good.” The subsequent prose allows development through a wife even without bodily support, so the poem’s valid negative is not automatically changed.

[^108]: **A damaged month restored.** S has 士子 beneath the Renyin year of the assistant-surveillance-commissioner case. G615 gives 壬子 for the same entry, supporting “[Renzi].” S’s 癸己 in the commander-in-chief entry is read as Guisi, with G615’s 癸巳 confirming the ordinary 己/巳 confusion. Fang Zhengliang’s 魁 indicates a high examination placing; no exact ordinal is supplied by S.

[^109]: **Conflicting verdicts on transformation.** The Dinghai paragraph says both 化氣凶, transformed qi is ominous, and, citing the Shenbai Classic, 化木主貴, transformation into Wood gives distinction. Both stand in S and G615. Several day entries favor Metal-and-Water luck although the final song excludes Metal: these alternative judgments are not recast as a single unconditional rule. Zu Dashou’s 忠臣, “loyal minister,” is likewise the source’s description, not a new historical verdict.

[^110]: **A deliberately contemptuous image.** Both S and G615 read 狗祿蠅名, literally a dog’s emolument and a fly’s name. “A cur’s stipend” and “a flyspeck of fame” preserve the belittling force without normalizing the phrase to the different wording found in juan six. Shen and Shang are the traditional separated stars evoked by 參商: here an image of estrangement among kin, not two additional natal indicators.

[^111]: **Examination years are not additional pillars.** The Jiyou/Xinwei grid has two captions, one for a man of She and one for a man of Fujian. Gengwu and Bingzi identify their examination years; they are not separate birth-year/month pairs. Both persons and both outcomes are retained. The statement about Zhao Kehuai’s death is also part of S’s biographical caption rather than an astrological conclusion added by the translator.

[^112]: **An invalid pair repaired from the parallel.** S begins the bureau-director case with 丙巳, which is not a valid sexagenary pair. G615 gives 乙巳甲申 for the corresponding Chen Yingzhi case, supporting “[Yisi] / Jiashen.” The personal name in G is not imported where S supplies only the office. The distant phrase 舉革 in S’s Bingwu/Bingshen case is retained as graduation followed by dismissal, rather than silently changed to G’s shorter caption.

[^113]: **Posthumous style, not a dukedom.** S calls Yang Jisheng 忠烈公, Lord Zhonglie; G615 reads 忠愍公, Lord Zhongmin, supporting the bracketed repair. 公 in this respectful name should not by itself be expanded into a claim that he held the peerage of duke. The remaining natal pair and office information are unchanged.

[^114]: **The narrow day in the closing quatrain remains narrow.** S and G615 begin the quatrain 丁丑日, specifically Dingchou, even though the section covers all six Ding days. The translation does not change it to a generic Ding day. The butterfly-dream title is a literary image of apparent prospects dissolving on waking; it supplies no separate dream-divination method.

[^115]: **Jia “within Chen.”** Both S and G615 explicitly place Jia Wood’s Seal-Ribbon in Chen. This is not the ordinary hidden-stem listing of Chen, and it has not been silently changed to Yi. The chapter also speaks of assembling a configuration and of stored qi; the consulted parallel does not establish a more precise explanation or a textual repair.

[^116]: **Two supported readings.** S’s 辰戊貴厚 is G615’s 辰戌貴厚, supporting Chen and “[Xu].” In the following Dingyou entry, S reads 年午月支, whereas G615 has 年干月支, supporting “[year stem] and monthly branch.” These repairs affect the prose rules, not any recorded birth pair. 檢討 is rendered as a Hanlin reviser, distinct from the 編修 compiler named elsewhere.

[^117]: **Officer’s and Wealth’s Storehouses are both named.** The short verse says 官庫 and the next quatrain 財庫; neither has been replaced by the other. The key is Xu’s clash with Chen, while another line asks for no punishment or breaking: the change of emphasis is preserved. “Money Dragon” renders 錢龍 as an image attached to the wealth-bearing Storehouse, not a new astronomical body.

[^118]: **A palace caption, not a direction to travel.** 天啟西宮 identifies the woman through the Tianqi reign and the Western Palace; it does not name a western sector of her natal chart. S supplies no personal name or precise consort title, and none is guessed. This and other late captions are reasons to distinguish the attributed compilation from the transmission history of its case collection.

[^119]: **A contrary family verdict and a lexical variant.** The last song promises reasonably peaceful parents and siblings and no anger or distress among wife and children, while individual day entries warn of injury. The general song does not erase those qualifications. S’s 財藝 is rendered wealth and skilled pursuits; G615 has 材藝, talent and skill. The source’s Ren–Geng–Xin–Gui sequence remains in its given order.

[^120]: **Owl is the transmitted reading.** Both S and G615 say 支無梟破, no Owl in the branches to break the arrangement, before naming Zi and Mao as the adverse branches. 梟 has not been silently replaced with 卯 to make the two clauses identical. The opening verse’s Rat and Rabbit are the animal names for Zi and Mao, not two additional influences.

[^121]: **Full captions, including their historical layering.** The Wang Sunfan case includes his earlier service at Jining and the local praise of his government, not merely his examination and office. The woman’s Dingsi example explicitly distinguishes having no children from receiving an honorific patent through an adopted son. These distinctions are retained. 甲部, also encountered elsewhere in the catalogue, remains an unresolved office label; it is not expanded into a guessed ministry.

[^122]: **The source itself disputes the imperial chart.** S lists Wuchen/Renxu under Dingchou day and Dingwei hour, then cites a dynastic-history date giving Bingzi day and Jichou hour and says that the historical record should prevail. G615 supplies the Hongwu chart without this extended correction. Neither version has been used to erase S’s disagreement. 明出地上, brightness coming forth above the earth, invokes the image of the Jin hexagram and makes a literary connection with the dynastic name Ming. It is the compiler or annotator’s argument, not an independently verified birth record.

[^123]: **Displaced typography, not displaced content.** In S, 月 is isolated inside small-character markup and 午 appears on the following line, interrupted by a case caption. Reading the continuous rule as Yin or Mao month, followed by Chen, Xu, Shen, and Wu, is supported by G615. The Jiawu/Renshen case remains with Dingmao day; its placement between prose lines is not a reason to omit it. 貲郎 is understood as an appointee through wealth qualification, not treated as a personal name.

[^124]: **Repeated chart, separate people.** The all-Dingwei catalogue expressly says one metropolitan graduate and one licentiate. Its earlier Dingsi-day list also includes a Dingwei/Dingwei year-and-month pair, which is not the same full chart. Neither the repeated pair nor differing attainments should be deduplicated. The claims about the named ministers, their sons, and their scholarly reputations are preserved as catalogue statements.

[^125]: **Geese and the opening Storehouse.** The ranks or companions of geese, 鴈侶, are an image for siblings, as 鴈行 is elsewhere. The song’s Chou–Wei clash opens the Storehouse and permits development. Other lines prohibit clash or predict an adverse end; these belong to particular entries and are not deleted to create an unconditional “clashes are beneficial” rule.

[^126]: **One missing branch restored.** In the Dingyou case grid S gives 癸亥 and only 戊 beneath it, immediately before the following Xinmao/Jiawu case. G615 has 癸亥戊午, supporting Guihai/[Wuwu]. The Ming Wuzong caption uses 透頂榮格 and 天闕地軸格 in S; G615 expands the first to 透頂連榮格 and gives 天關 rather than 天闕 in the second. S’s configuration names and complete branch sequence are retained.

[^127]: **The long closing song is absent from S.** This section ends in S after the four-line verse beginning 丁日戊申時為正. G615 continues with an eight-line song: “A Ding day at Wushen hour is the proper form; / Upon Shen, Wu Earth reaches Birth. / Hurting Officer with Officer wholly removed finds passage amid obstruction; / Guihai filling the position is forbidden in use. / The gentleman withdraws and relinquishes office; / The common person finds his livelihood hard to establish. / Should wealth and distinction leave no contentment with poverty, / Suspect that the birth hour was not correctly fixed.” This is supplied here as a parallel-witness addition, not silently inserted into the controlling text. S’s shorter closing verse is complete as transmitted.

[^128]: **“One character, two combinations.”** The Dingmao entry names Hai and Wei for their Wood affiliation with Mao, and Si and Chou for their Metal affiliation with You, then states that a single character brings two combinations. That compressed explanation is retained without inventing a missing diagram. The Lord Yang caption’s three Noble Persons and three sha are likewise its own enumeration; the text does not name all six individually here.

[^129]: **A colloquial adjective amid literary diction.** 響快 in S and G615 is understood as frankness and good cheer rather than literally “sounding fast”; the wording may represent the familiar 爽快. This is contextual interpretation, not an attested graphic correction. Wealth and Officer in Double Beauty is the name used by the song, even though the opening develops Food and Wealth.

[^130]: **Bing within Xu is not replaced by Ding.** S and G615 both say 戌中有丙, Xu contains Bing, in explaining Defeat. That is not the ordinary hidden-stem assignment of Xu. The translation retains the transmitted Bing and does not silently substitute a modern table. The consequent warnings about the eyes remain historical prognoses, not medical findings.

[^131]: **A difficult two-person grid.** S places 甲午 at the start of the Cheng Zhengyi line and 甲戌 at the start of the Yu Huaizhong line, with examination years and biographical captions running between them. The translation provisionally reads Jiawu/Jiaxu as the shared year/month pair of two cases, following the grid’s year-over-month convention. Wuwu, Dingmao, and Xinwei are kept as examination-year references, Bingxu as a death year, and Guisi with Yu’s advancement. G615 does not contain these added cases and therefore cannot confirm the reconstruction. No second natal year or month has been invented; the original layout should be consulted before treating the shared-chart reading as settled.

[^132]: **Two transmitted hours for Yin Wuqing.** S places Dinghai/Gengxu with the name Yin Wuqing under both the present Gengxu-hour section and the following Xinhai-hour section. Here he is called a minister and the account is attributed to astrological case records; there he is a chief censor and the caption says “the original text transmits it.” Both versions remain. Their duplication does not establish which hour is historically correct.

[^133]: **The closed Storehouse can preserve wealth.** The last song says 庫閉財能收貯, a closed Storehouse can gather and keep wealth. That positive statement is retained, although the surrounding verses lament the lack of a key and delayed development. “Depending on the wife” renders the household arrangement described by 就合托妻隨住 rather than adding a claim that every such person is adopted as a son-in-law.

[^134]: **A name corrected, an unnamed entry left unnamed.** S’s 趙炳官 corresponds to G615’s 趙炳然, supporting Zhao [Bingran]. In the same list S supplies Bingwu/Jiawu without a caption. G615 identifies that pair as a metropolitan graduate named Liu Risheng, but that additional identification is not silently imported into the controlling text. The bare year/month pair remains explicitly marked as unnamed in S.

[^135]: **Separate branches, not a Jihai pillar.** S reads 辛月得己亥; G615 has 辛月得巳亥. The corresponding rule is therefore translated as a Xin-stem month with [Si] and Hai, not a requirement for a Jihai month. The alternative verdict 濁, “turbid,” stands beside manifest refinement and is not harmonized away. 馬頭帶劍 is the transmitted named configuration in Hong Fuzhong’s caption, not an account of his actual equipment.

[^136]: **Shared fate does not mean an identical biography.** S differentiates Rao Cai and Wan Lai by examination level, region, children, and banishment. 萬來 is retained as Wan Lai here; related discussions elsewhere in the book give 萬宷, Wan Cai. G615 also has 萬來 at this point, so it does not establish a repair. The two hours supplied for Yin Wuqing are discussed in the preceding section’s note; no duplicate has been discarded.

[^137]: **Kun, Peng, and the rising scholar.** 鯤 and 鵬 name the great fish and bird of the Zhuangzi’s opening image. The verse also turns a moulting rooster into a phoenix, joining images of transformation with examination and official advancement. 寒門 means a humble household; it does not itself mean a specific low caste. 終養 in Zhu Yibin’s caption indicates remaining with parents to care for them through their remaining years.

[^138]: **An annual prohibition among monthly prohibitions.** S and G615 both say 乙卯年, Yimao year, followed by Guisi and Bingwu months. The year has not been silently changed into a month to make the list uniform. The general prose also distinguishes Wu remaining Earth from Wu combining with Gui to transform into Fire; the same Water cannot be evaluated identically under both readings.

[^139]: **An incomplete case restored from G615.** S’s first Wuyin caption preserves only 選侍郎, 丁酉, and 原本傳. G615 supplies 洪朝選侍郎丙子丁酉, supporting Vice Minister [Hong Chaoxuan], [Bingzi]/Dingyou. The restored portions are bracketed. The same person appears in the preceding Dingchou/Xinhai material with a different day and hour; that difference is retained as a transmission problem, not concealed by consolidating the two entries.

[^140]: **An unresolved line.** Both S and G615 read 化為丁丁是漫漫. The literal “transformed into Ding, Ding stretches boundlessly” is retained in quotation marks because the grammar and the specific role of the repeated 丁 are uncertain. The first prose paragraph does discuss Fire transformation, but that does not recover the verse’s exact wording. 萬貫 is the conventional image of ten thousand strings of cash, not a modern currency amount or a verified estate valuation.

[^141]: **Xu month restored; an alternative hour preserved.** S’s 戊月 in the Wuzi rule is G615’s 戌月, supporting “[Xu] month.” S’s Wang Ao caption gives Jiayin as the alternative hour; G615 gives Jiashen. The English preserves S’s Jiayin and notes G’s different reading here. Both alternatives differ from the section’s Guichou hour. 羅文貴 is rendered Net-Pattern Nobility as a technical name, not reduced to the ordinary stem combination alone.

[^142]: **The source asks for flexible comparison.** This instruction follows all six day entries, and therefore applies to their combined year/month judgments, not just to Wuxu. It is retained as a check against using the compact formulas in isolation. The Noble Consort’s Wuchen/Yichou pair remains under Wuxu day and Guichou hour; no personal identity is supplied by S. Xie Xuelong’s manner of death and repeated misfortunes are the case caption’s historical assertions.

[^143]: **A meaningful negative retained.** In the Wuzi entry S has 午月無制, Wu month without restraint, whereas G615 has 午月有制, with restraint. S’s valid negative is retained, rather than mechanically made consistent with the surrounding rules. The general method requires attention to whether the body or Killings is stronger; neither the translation nor this note supplies a missing set of conditions that would conclusively reconcile the witnesses.

[^144]: **Knife and brush.** 刀筆 evokes administrative and especially legal drafting, not battlefield weapons. The passage praises that skill while warning against excessive restraint of Killings. In the accompanying Tang Xiaochun and Han Yinglong captions, Jichou and Yiwei are examination years and Dingyou a death year, not extra natal pillars. Those dates and the explicitly early deaths remain attached to their respective people.

[^145]: **A redundant Zi resolved by the parallel.** S has 申子酉子年月; G615 reads 申子酉字年月. The last 子 is read as 字, “character,” giving the three branches Shen, Zi, and You rather than inventing a fourth branch or imposing two Zi. The catalogue’s Eastern Palace consort is not assigned a personal identity absent from S.

[^146]: **Jiayin, not “Shenyin.”** S begins the last song 戊日申寅時; G615 has 戊日甲寅時, agreeing with the section, the general prose, and all six entries. The bracketed Jiayin is a supported repair. 假煞反為權印 means that employing or borrowing Killings can turn it into a seal of authority; 權印 here need not designate an additional natal Seal Star.

[^147]: **Moral judgment remains attributed.** S calls Yan Maoqing a minister and accuses him of siding with the wicked and harming the good before banishment. G615 calls him a vice minister. The translation retains S’s office and censure as catalogue statements, not independently established historical findings. “Two Stems Unmixed” describes the repeated Wu and Yi across the full Wuchen/Yimao/Wuchen/Yimao chart; it is not a general claim that the two stems are incapable of combining with others.

[^148]: **An opaque two-character caption.** S’s Yiyou/Wuyin case is labelled 濫獨. “Dissolute and alone” retains a possible literal sense, but the caption is not secure; G615 omits this additional case. It is not converted into a named crime, an office, or a missing personal name. The valid birth pair is preserved independently of that uncertainty.

[^149]: **A difficult combination of images.** The song juxtaposes 木衝六合, Wood’s clash and Sixfold Combination, with Golden Rooster and Jade Rabbit, You and Mao. The obvious opposition of the two branches does not authorize replacing the reference to combination. 乙酉辛傷 is read as Yi, You, and Xin bringing injury, while 鴈侶 and 重花結子 retain the family images of siblings, repeated flowering, and offspring. The consulted G615 text has the same wording and does not establish a clearer reconstruction.

[^150]: **Mao and Xu, not a substituted clash pair.** S’s final song names 卯戌 as releasing Yi and Gui from the Storehouse. The translation preserves Mao and Xu rather than changing Mao to Chen for the sake of a familiar Chen–Xu clash. The introductory short verse invokes 否 and 泰, Obstruction and Peace, as the reversal from adversity to passage. Inverted Food remains the hour’s Bing relation even while Wealth and Officer are the treasures being released.

[^151]: **An explicit exception to the opening prohibition.** The Wuxu entry states that Returning Lu does not avoid Officer, Killings, and Wealth when bodily strength makes them beneficial. This qualifies, rather than cancels, the opening account that excludes them. The Wuwu entry also permits changing the analytical configuration to Officer and Seal when those influences appear. The author’s readiness to change the category is part of the method, not a textual inconsistency to suppress.

[^152]: **Yu’s Gate and examination success.** 禹門, the three-tiered waves, the cassia, and the Toad Palace combine images of the fish’s upward leap and a scholar’s advancement. The translation preserves the images while the context identifies their promise of official success. The final instruction to seek another configuration when breaking occurs is translated explicitly; the verse is not a guarantee that Returning Lu applies to every Wu day at Dingsi hour.

[^153]: **Year versus Wu branch.** S says 年中火旺, Fire flourishing in the year; G615 reads 午中火旺, Fire flourishing within Wu. S is retained because either construction is intelligible, although G supplies a closer reference to the hour’s branch. 夏不鎔鑄 in the Wuzi entry, “in summer it is not smelted and cast,” is also shared by the witnesses and remains an unresolved metaphor rather than being replaced by an invented technical condition.

[^154]: **The missing end of a name.** S preserves 王子 and the office 中丞 with a gap between them. G615 gives 王子勉中丞 with Guiwei/Xinyou, supporting Wang Zi[mian]. G615 places the Bingwu/Xinmao provincial-first case under Wuyin, whereas S places that pair under Wuzi. The translation follows S’s placement and does not move the example merely because the parallel differs.

[^155]: **Filling the position is favorable here.** The Wushen entry explicitly calls Wei filling the position and Si’s Established Lu linked into a sequence greatly distinguished. That is different from the general warnings against filling an arched position elsewhere in the book. It has not been “corrected” into a prohibition. The following alternative prediction of execution or death in battle is also retained, including the source’s example of Shen Lian.

[^156]: **Stem Blade and stored Wood.** Ji is called Wu’s Yang Blade, and the visible hour stem and hidden Ji within Wei are counted as two Blades. This is a stem-based extension of Blade terminology, not two occurrences of a Wu branch. The reference to Jia Wood within Wei’s combination is retained even though a standard hidden-stem list names Yi there. G615 agrees and gives no secure correction. “Within twenty years” translates the transmitted parental-loss prediction without claiming a reliable modern age calculation.

[^157]: **Valid but different natal pairs are not repaired by majority rule.** S’s first Wuyin case reads Bingwu/Jiawu, a minister; G615 instead names Minister Guo Qian with Xinwei/Jiawu. Both year/month pairs are possible, so S’s is retained. Similarly, Lin Yingliang appears here under Jiwei hour and in the earlier Guichou-hour section with the same Bingyin/Yiwei pair. The edition’s alternative hours remain visible rather than being silently consolidated.

[^158]: **The comparison of Wang Kentang and Xiang Dexiang is complete.** S supplies Wang’s medical compilations, absence of sons, and Jintan origin, then contrasts Xiang’s lower examination rank, county office, sons, and Shangyuan origin despite the shared chart. 證治準繩 is rendered *Standards of Diagnosis and Treatment*; it is the title in the caption, not medical advice or a newly authenticated bibliography. G615 lacks this extended comparison, so its shorter list has not replaced S.

[^159]: **One branch supplied from G615.** S says 二寅不怕一衝, two Yin do not fear one clash, leaving a space before 衝. G615 has 二寅不怕一申衝, supporting the supplied “[Shen].” Other valid case variants remain unchanged: S’s Gengwu/Wuzi distinguished case, for example, differs from G615’s Gengwu/Wuwu. The presence of a more familiar pair in a parallel is not by itself proof that S is wrong.

[^160]: **High walls are a sentence, not a birthplace.** 發高牆 in the Prince of Yi’s caption denotes dispatch to confinement, translated without supplying a location not named here. The Shao Mei entry’s Xinsi/Bingshen in S differs from G615’s Dingsi/Bingshen; S’s valid pair is retained. The two sons’ metropolitan degrees belong to the father’s biographical caption, not to additional chart rows.

[^161]: **An unresolved opening fragment.** S and G615 both transmit 災瘧內子月. The translation provisionally separates 災瘧內, “calamity and intermittent fever within,” from the following Zi-month judgment, but the function of 內 is unclear. It could belong to damaged wording about the wife or household; the parallel does not establish that reading. No modern diagnosis is inferred from 瘧, and no missing sentence is invented.

[^162]: **Food generating blessing.** S has 食神生神, Food God generates spirit; G615 reads 食神生福, Food God generates blessing. The bracketed “blessing” follows the parallel and the stanza’s favorable sense. The images of damaged branches and leaves and the ranks of geese evoke family injury; they are not instructions to prune plants or interpret actual bird movements.

[^163]: **A different hour remains an alternative.** The Qian Fu caption explicitly gives Jiayin as an alternative to this section’s Xinyou hour. That alternative agrees with G615 and is retained without selecting one as historically established. “Later life bears no fruit” translates 晚無結果 broadly: the source can apply fruition to livelihood and offspring, so it should not automatically be reduced to an exact prediction of childlessness.

[^164]: **The compiler’s generalization conflicts with his catalogue.** The assertion that the six days have no fate of a great official stands beside examples labelled ministers, vice ministers, and a chancellor. G615 also preserves this tension. The translation does not delete the assertion, demote the named offices, or add a condition not in the text. Wu Zhenying’s 問死免 means a death sentence from which he was spared, not that he died and revived.

[^165]: **The negative is retained.** Both S and G615 say 無破難招祖業, without breaking it is difficult to obtain ancestral property. This is not silently changed to “with breaking,” even though the opening fears damage. In this book, breaking an obstruction and damaging a useful influence are not automatically the same judgment; the line itself supplies no further distinction.

[^166]: **A corrupted hour heading corrected by the entire section.** S’s heading reads 戊戌, Wuxu, but its introductory poem, general prose, all six day entries, and closing song say 壬戌, Renxu. G615 likewise heads the section 壬戌. The English therefore uses Renxu, while the Chinese heading displays both the transmitted form and the correction. This is not an alternative thirteenth hour.

[^167]: **One conjectural repair and one supported repair.** S and G615 both say 戌以壬為財, Xu takes Ren as Wealth. “[Wu]” is a conjectural restoration of 戊 for 戌: the statement concerns the Wu day stem, and the next clause already treats the Xu branch. In the Wuzi catalogue, however, S’s impossible 甲戊 month is G615’s 甲戌, supporting Jiashen/[Jiaxu]. The two changes are deliberately assigned different evidential status.

[^168]: **Case captions do not override the general rule.** S includes poor and childless graduates and a violently executed man among the Renxu-hour examples. The favorable autumn rule is not a warrant to suppress those outcomes. 磔死 is rendered execution by dismemberment without graphic elaboration. No name has been imported from G615’s longer caption for that man.

[^169]: **The household phrase is compressed.** 開庫填房就舍 is rendered “open the Storehouse, fill a vacant chamber, and join a household.” 填房 can refer to occupying a vacant marital position, while 就舍 suggests establishing oneself in an existing household. The text is too brief to determine a precise legal or kinship arrangement, and the translation does not impose one. S’s 出住 is understood as going forth or staying; G615 has the more conventional 出處. 無衝鴈侶難完, without clash the sibling group is hard to keep whole, is retained rather than reversed.

[^170]: **Xu and Chou are transmitted, not inferred.** Both S and G615 read 化火無戌 and 乙庚丁丑無. The poem is therefore translated with Xu absent in the first clause and Chou absent in the next, while Yi, Geng, and Ding appear. Replacing 戌 with 成 to say that transformation fails would be conjecture, not a recovered reading from the consulted parallel. The general prose does expressly say the Fire image fails in Water’s country; that contextual sense is not silently substituted into the verse.

[^171]: **A prohibition and an exception without an explanation.** The Wuzi rule excludes Wu in year and month, yet immediately gives Chief Censor Li Wenjin’s Wuchen/Jiwei as a case. G615 preserves both. No additional condition is supplied to reconcile them. S’s subsequent Shen-or-Zi rule differs from G615’s 甲子, Jiazi; the translation keeps S’s 申子 rather than selecting the alternate merely for uniformity.

[^172]: **The juan ends here.** The final quatrain, eight-line song, closing volume label, and Siku classification are all represented. The translation does not continue into the Ji-day entries, which belong to juan nine. Its image of late brightness after sunset and the final promise of wife and children stand alongside the earlier family-injury clauses; those changes in register and judgment are retained.
